DAMNED

WIELD POWER AMONG THE KINDRED
For the newly embraced Kindred, finding a place amid the changing landscape of titles and sects is crucial. Princes, primogen, barons, hierophants, and other leaders loom over each city, and in their shadows the rest of the Kindred make havens for themselves and eke out their domains and herds. Once free from their sires, neonates and ancillae alike must learn to navigate the twisted halls of power.
Courts of the Damned unveils the political landscape found in most Kindred cities. Whether vying for territory and blood among the Anarchs, the Camarilla, or independent domains, the stories of clashes between young and old, tyranny and freedom, or faith and reason repeat themselves across the globe. Whether the domain is small or large, isolated or in close contact with others, all Kindred must vie for blood, territory, and autonomy in their own eternal struggle.
Courts of the Damned provides: · Guidance for portraying leaders and power players in your city, as a player or the Storyteller · Strategies for navigating the ruthless schemes of the undead, grasping the resources Kindred fight over, and clawing your way to the top · Expanded options for social combat to embarrass a rival or gain titles and status · A deep dive into the internal and external feuds of Anarchs, Camarilla, and independent factions · 4 new Loresheets and suggestions for using established lore (and Loresheets) in play · 6 new Discipline Powers, 3 new Blood Sorcery Rituals, and 1 new Oblivion Ceremony · 6 frameworks for a city full of political intrigue · 5 easy-to-run stories full of plotting and scheming · Traits for 16 prominent Storyteller characters, including Victoria Ash, Theo Bell, Salvatore Garcia, Fiorenza Savona, and Tegyrius, with 21 generic, influential Storyteller characters
Courts of the Damned is a guide to politics for Vampire: The Masquerade, a Storytelling Game of Personal and Political Horror.

Yui: Thank you for being so gracious with me, Ms. Ash.
Victoria Ash: It's nothing, and please call me Victoria. They're just scared of you because they know what your sire has done. There are so many disturbing stories about the Tzimisce flitting about.
Yui: Yes, Victoria. So I've heard. But I'm still thankful. I want to make something of myself here, and any help...
Victoria Ash: Oh, don't beg. Let's reserve that for the bedchamber. You want me to be your shadow... an advisor.
Yui: Yes, please.
Victoria Ash: Let's look at you. You look sweet, you know how to dress for the Elysium, and your clan gives you a frisson of danger.
Yui: Thank you...
Victoria Ash: But let me tell you something about myself. In a way, I detest the Tzimisce. I feel your whole damnable clan has been put on this Earth to test me. I've dealt with it in my own way. I've betrayed Tzimisce, romanced, destroyed... I don't like it when someone gets under my skin, literally or figuratively. My skin is perfect as it is.
Yui: I don't understand.
Victoria Ash: So, if I'm going to help you, I'm afraid that'll come with a cost. If bad memories bedevil me, I might just feel the urge to take it out on the nearest Tzimisce.
Yui: You should be careful, Ms. Ash. Maybe the nearest Tzimisce is going to make you beg instead.
Victoria Ash: Oh, darling. I like that. Now, I've promised some of my compatriots that they'll get to taste Tzimisce blood tonight. I trust you won't make a fuss. Don't worry--I'll stop them before they take too much.
 A conversation at the Elysium between the Toreador elder Victoria Ash and the Tzimisce newcomer Yui

Introduction

INTRODUCTION

Politics happens when people want things. More specifically, when people want power--power to make decisions for themselves or others, power to amass wealth, territory, and resources--and can't or won't use outright force to acquire it. Whether it's at the gang parley or the PTA meeting, would-be politicians negotiate with, intimidate, and flatter each other to get what they need.
The vampire is a political creature. In Vampire: The Masquerade, the undead plot and scheme to gain influence, avenge slights (real and imagined), and keep themselves busy after decades and centuries of boredom. Their machinations have horrific costs to those around them, but that's just part of the game. In the politics of the dead, politeness and brutality go hand in hand.
Characters in Vampire are embroiled in politics, whether or not they want to be. The demands of keeping their predatory activities secret, coupled with their ageless existence, mean they must constantly navigate relationships with one another

and with select mortals to keep the Masquerade intact and their prey in the dark. This is especially true in the context of the Beckoning and the newly invigorated Second Inquisition, leaving long-occupied Kindred strongholds and political positions vacant and in disarray. The political game has never been as open to newcomers, nor the stakes so high.
This book is a resource for creating and playing a political chronicle. For players, it's a place to find advice on making alliances, embarrassing enemies, and navigating Camarilla, Anarch, and autark political spheres. For Storytellers, this book provides support for creating and facilitating political roleplaying at the table: rules, dice pools, stories, and more.
What does it mean to make a political play? What are the stakes the undead fight over? How do you run stories about politics? Answering questions like these makes it easier to implement politics successfully in your game, not only as a theme but also as a set of actions.

6

They say that the Kindred are poison to any community we're part of, but it doesn't have to be like that. It's always a choice. We're responsible for our actions just like mortals are. We're no better or more evil than any other living creature. ("We're undead," someone shouts.) Okay. Fine, if you want to be superstitious, then yes, the Blood has cursed us for eternity, and there is no hope for us. But personally, I want to think I can do some good. Not just for myself but for the mortals. ("Like hunt Nazis," someone mutters.) Yes. Vampires must feed. That unites all of us, and we can choose what to do with that. But you should be careful with drinking from those you hate because associating with them too much might start changing you before you notice. One night, you're feeding from a skinhead, and the next, you're protecting him because you need your herd to survive. ("Come on, Rudi," a voice protests.) I know, believe me. There are no easy answers. I wish there were. Being a vampire means that we have to be part of both the societies of the living and the dead, and we have to consider how our actions affect both. Though the fossils of the Camarilla would never say it: everything changes. For good or ill, that's up to us.
 The Gangrel Rudi holds forth at an Anarch assembly
7

COURTS OF THE DAMNED

Introduction

Stability and Chaos
There's a dichotomy between what's happening in the setting of Vampire in general and what works for the specific setting of a chronicle. Although the World of Darkness is in conflict and flux, most domains enjoy periods of calm interrupted by moments of strife. Typically, a political chronicle works best if it is set in one of the domains that happen to be in turmoil.
Instability creates opportunities for players' characters to take risks. This is why so many Vampire chronicles start with the sudden murder, disappearance, or attempted abdication of the Prince (such are much the same as suicide). The political vacuum created invites everyone to make a play for it. If you wish to imagine a successful domain, it makes sense to picture a just, capable Prince leading

a loyal Primogen Council in maintaining the proud Traditions of the Camarilla. But if you want to design a more playable domain, injecting chaos does the trick. Suddenly, the Prince is an incompetent figurehead, elevated to their position because the Primogen thought they'd be easy to control, while upstarts from every corner of Kindred society move to destabilize the domain for their own advantage.
When it comes to politics, vampires fortunately suffer from all the sins of humanity, often to a heightened degree. Greed, envy, contempt, and suspicion drive even the most seemingly idealistic Anarch ideologue. As does Hunger. Sometimes, the political systems of the Kindred collapse for no greater reason than that someone chose to hunt in a territory claimed by one of their peers. The tension between strategy and beastly impulses drives Kindred politics, as you will find detailed throughout this book.

MATURE CONTENT WARNING Vampire: The Masquerade is a game of personal and political horror where you portray a bloodsucking monster in a world resembling our own. Characters routinely experience (and participate in) activities typical of vampire fiction: violence, seduction, exploitation, deceit, and murder.
This book, in particular, is about how the undead scheme to exploit the systems they operate within, involving themes such as political corruption, organized crime, warfare, conspiracies, and harming others for your own potential benefit. Furthermore, this is a book that centers a playstyle where players' characters could end up targeting each other in competing for status and power. Please take the time to negotiate limits for play in advance. See Tips for Considerate Social Conflict (p. 21) and Advice for Considerate Play in Vampire: The Masquerade (p. 421).

8

COURTS OF THE DAMNED

Chapters
This book is divided into six chapters that examine politics from different angles. The first four chapters and appendix, the bulk of this work, are intended for both players and Storytellers alike. Everyone at the table can gain a better understanding of how to play politics through these chapters. Chapter Five focuses on the Storyteller's point of view, but players may find value in reading it as well, particularly for a troupe that collaboratively builds their chronicle. Only Chapter Six is primarily intended for Storytellers.
Chapter One: Playing Politics addresses the core question of how to approach political play across the whole troupe. What kinds of plots work well in a roleplaying game, what can you achieve, and how can you be successful with your stratagems? Along the way, the chapter explains the basic groundwork of vampire politics by examining topics like what's at stake (spoiler: blood) and how Kindred relations factor into the political landscape of the night. Social combat mechanics and a guide for session zero get you ready to start playing.
Chapter Two: The Ivory Tower looks at the Camarilla--for many vampires, the main stage of undead politics. Camarilla Kindred have the money, power, and influence to make their machinations affect large numbers of people. Understanding how the system works is your first step to making a move.

Chapter Three: The Movement is about the Anarchs in their variety. The Anarch Movement is fundamentally revolutionary, which means that the difference between idealism and reality is often on stark display. Although Anarch politics lack the formality of a Camarilla Court, they're deadly in their own way.
Chapter Four: Outside the Gates reminds us that beyond the great sects, there are the autarkis, Kindred who refuse to pledge themselves to any sect. Despite their independence, they, too, remain unable to escape political schemes that often take strange and unusual forms in unique domains.
Chapter Five: The Storyteller's Manifesto explains how to bring all of this together as the Storyteller and ensure personal horror remains at the forefront of political stories. The chapter also examines major events in the setting of Vampire and how to integrate them into your chronicle.
Chapter Six: Characters and Stories presents character types you can use to populate your political landscape, as well as stories that show how vampire politics can play out in practice. You can use everything here as is or adapt it to your chronicle.
Finally, the Appendix: Systems presents new Discipline Powers, Loresheets, and other game mechanics designed to help your character play politics in the world of the dead.

9

Chapter One:
PLAYING POLITICS
L et's say your character is a Ventrue, Embraced for their hard-won expertise from years running mortal election campaigns. They're a political animal through and through. Or maybe you're playing a fledgling Anarch who has realized your best chance of making it semi-safely through the night is to advance as fast as possible in the gang hierarchy of your home city.
Maybe you're a middle manager in a Hecata autarky who dreams only of escaping the job you've endured in the family firm for the last 50 years.
No matter your position in the hierarchies of the Kindred, this chapter is written to help you understand what kinds of moves create interesting political roleplay. It explores what's at stake, examines how Kindred power structures typically operate, and offers strategies suited for the scheming vampire. For the Storyteller, this chapter helps clarify what players are aiming for when they engage with politics. Once you know their goals, it's easier to craft meaningful challenges and opportunities.
Finally, you'll find advice for the whole troupe on setting up a political chronicle, complete with a political landscape ripe for exploration and exploitation. Mechanics for social combat and the Political Map, with its key movers and shakers, get you ready to bribe rivals, blackmail the powerful, and make false promises to the naïve.
You know, vampire politics.
11

Chapter One: Playing Politics

THE STAKES

Much of political conduct concerns itself with resources: who gets what, how much they get, and when they get it. The beginning of most resource negotiations is simple. Person A wants X. Person B has X. They talk.
What comes next is less a straightforward economic exchange--one X for two Y--and more a delicate balancing act of personalities, assets, and circumstances. Person B might hand off X to Person A for a pittance if they believe it'll make them look generous, or ask for more than X is worth because they know A has no alternatives. Or perhaps they outright refuse to give anything to A, even if the deal is advantageous to both, because they despise them.
For vampires, as with mortals, there's a distinct hierarchy of resources based on their necessity for survival. How much a vampire values a particular resource under normal circumstances may have little to do with how much her own survival depends on it--until she reaches the breaking point and cannot go on without it. A hungry vampire yearns to feed, but she can push past her own instincts if someone she cares about (a Touchstone, a childe, a lover) is at risk. Without that, the only thing on a starving vampire's mind is blood.
Blood
Everybody has got to eat. Big cities are the best place for Kindred because there's simply more mortal blood in one place. Do the math: one coterie of five careful vampires is going to feed a minimum of approximately 920 times each year. Assuming they're not Farmers and they don't have a Herd, the coterie probably can't risk feeding from the same

person more than once or twice in a year. If there are a few hundred Kindred in a city, drawn by all the nice warm blood pumping away, the prospective number of vampire victims per year shoots up into the tens of thousands.
Mistakes need to be avoided, attention kept to a minimum. Mortals must be unaware of Kindred boundaries and authorities, and there's no way to silo all the blood in an area into one place. The Masquerade exists for this very reason. It's only with careful management that everyone can eat.
Usually, the responsibility for protecting Kindred access to blood falls on a domain's leadership, who grant or revoke feeding rights and enforce the laws created to ensure that access. But every vampire, powerful and weak alike, has a vested interest in making sure that they can feed. To some, that means preserving order; to others, that means toppling a leadership that stands between them and the kine.
Shelter
Kindred slumber for approximately 12 hours each day, meaning they need a Haven (or at the very least a box to curl up in) to avoid being exposed to sunlight or mortals. The more secure the enclosed space, the better the chance to avoid a lethal accident. Kindred control shelter mostly by owning property. Cash greases the palms of city inspectors, police, and maintenance workers to keep the Kindred comfortable and safely stowed away during the day. In other words, shelter equals money, or vice versa.
Most Kindred need some source of cash to keep a Haven's roof over their head and clothes on their

12

COURTS OF THE DAMNED

back. In the modern age, that means having a bank account, maybe multiple if you want to do some money laundering. A good Mask is crucial. A Retainer who can manage finances is even better, especially if they have experience covering up fraud. For vampires, Resources aren't necessarily financial, but they often end up expressed in dollar signs--which means accruing Resources can net better shelter, security, and comfort.
Unfortunately (or fortunately, depending on whom you ask), the interconnected nature of finances, Masks, and Havens in tonight's world makes them ripe for attack. One good hack or a well-placed audit can have a Kindred scrambling to reconstruct their entire world with no attention for anything else.

Force
Force--physical violence--is the big red button of politics. Almost every undead ruler in power tonight achieved their position through calculated use of violence, and most maintain it by reminding their subjects of their capacity for it. If a ruler is particularly skilled in Fortitude or Potence, they might personally lead displays of force against their opponents. But every successful ruler, regardless of their capabilities, knows they need trusted enforcers to project that threat across their domain. The stronger the enforcers, the more potent the threat. Some rulers prioritize enforcers accomplished in certain Disciplines, placing them in key positions within their Court. Others embed their soldiers in mortal systems of power, trying to steer them to serve Kindred needs. A vampire in the police system or coast guard can wield the sunlit world as a weapon. Disrupting access to force can defang even
the most established ruler. Create enough chaos, and their power weakens as they scramble to deal with multiple problems. Remove or turn trusted enforcers, and a ruler's control crumbles rapidly.
Information
Secrecy is paramount. Information--the control of it, as well as the monitoring of its channels--is as vital to a domain's security as its enforcers. Like blood, information security is an unwieldy resource, constantly moving and changing. Those who specialize in it are more like fishermen than farmers, casting wide nets to catch fragments from the current.

13

Chapter One: Playing Politics

The internet is the source of most knowledge these nights. There's still plenty of room for Kindred and mortal informants with eyes and ears to pick up rumors around a city, but the vast majority of seekers in the information trade now hunch over computers and phones, typing furiously. Most domains maintain a nominal ban on technology more advanced than a flip phone, though Kindred within their borders often stretch this rule. Some rulers employ dedicated enforcers to track down unauthorized devices; others turn a blind eye. Every

savvy ruler knows the price of missing the right piece of intel--a visit from the Second Inquisition ranks high on no one's wish list.
Special dispensations to access computers-- "technology allowance" in the formal parlance, "plugging in" in the casual, or "getting my phone back" among the newly Embraced--may be granted to certain Kindred within a domain. Plugging in can be a job, serving as a digital proxy for a Prince or Anarch boss and maintaining the Masquerade, or it might be bestowed as a reward or political favor.

 Salvador Garcia, Brujah ideologue
14

COURTS OF THE DAMNED

POLITICAL FRAMEWORKS

While Kindred society can take countless forms, a handful of archetypal frameworks for how it can be structured recur time and time again in domains worldwide. Anarchs and Camarilla replicate these structures independently of each other, and there are examples of all the below on both the Ivory Tower's and the Revolt's sides of the chessboard.
No matter the model, these political frameworks touch kine governments unobtrusively and only as much as necessary. Even a small city has hundreds, if not thousands, of mortal government workers intersecting with each other all the time. No vampire can control a city's whole mortal administration without endangering the Masquerade--especially given the ever-watching eyes of the internet and the Second Inquisition. Kindred leaders can't afford to wield an iron fist without covering it with a velvet glove. A generous donation to the mayor's campaign and a charming request ensure a particular historic building remains untouched by rezoning; offering a connection to the ambitious alderman encourages him to tip a vote in one direction or the other. Kindred usually navigate their own politics in insular domains beneath these machinations.
In a collaborative or troupe-driven chronicle, everyone might review these frameworks and collectively pick an option that makes the most sense. In a Storyteller-driven chronicle, the Storyteller may choose one of these options and ask the players to create characters that fit the chosen option.

The Monarchy
The Monarchy is as close to a standard political framework as the Kindred have--a city ruled by an absolute feudal monarch and their advisors. The Monarch's decrees are enforced by the Kindred to whom they have afforded titles or privileges, whether they're called Sheriffs, Seneschals, or something else. Like kingdoms of yore, the Monarchy is rich in territory, status, and violence. Its weaknesses lie in the loyalty of its enforcers and the potential for its territory to stagnate. A Monarchy is usually a place of history and formality, proud of the pomp and circumstance surrounding its proceedings.
The draw of the Monarch's rule is that very formality: petitioners or visitors know exactly where to present themselves and to whom to show deference. Local laws may differ based on a Monarch's latitude, but in Camarilla Monarchies the Six Traditions (Vampire, p. 51) take precedence. Punishments are typically brutal, rewards generous: blood, gifts, titles, or financial assets. Those in the highest echelons of the Monarch's city receive the most. Those in the lowest corners can expect the least.
It would be easy to assume the Monarch's city is Camarilla by default, but Anarch petty tyrants can be just as restrictive and prone to accidentally reproducing Camarilla culture as they tighten their grip on a region.

15

Chapter One: Playing Politics

In Chronicles
The Monarchy clashes with the Assembly and may find common ground or an occasional partnership with the Collegium. It despises the Caligula as a failed Monarchy.
Succession Crisis: A Monarch met final death, and there's no clear successor. Their old allies all back different candidates, and the conflict threatens to erupt into citywide violence.
Viva la Revolution: A group of revolutionaries wants to overthrow the Monarchy. They ask the players' characters to be their guerrilla agents, disrupting the Monarch's plans and fighting her enforcers. But there's a mole among the radicals.
The Favorite: It's the anniversary of the Monarch's reign, and her Court gathers in Elysium to celebrate. To everyone's surprise, an old favorite of hers returns for the event. The Monarch's new favorite believes he's trying to unseat her.
The Firm
It's common to mistake the Firm for a Monarchy at first glance--the Director ruling over a company of underlings, assisted by a powerful Board of executives, looks an awful lot like a Monarch and their domain. But there is a touch more modernity in its night-to-night operation (or so it claims).
A Firm tries to emulate the corporations it takes its cues from. Most decisions are voted on by the Board, who command lower-ranking Kindred and rake in the cash. Meanwhile, the Director breaks ties in meetings, presides over operations, and makes alliances. Underlings are given tasks roughly based on merit and skill, and there's a promise of upward mobility for successful followers. Of course, the reality of advancement depends entirely on the whims of the Director and the Board, and a disliked underling will not advance half as fast as their lessskilled but more popular peer.
The ostensible goals of a Firm tend to be fiscal or technological. There's a literal business at the core of the Firm's operations, with both mortal and Kindred workers who have bought into the

long-term investment strategy of immortality. This allows a certain amount of built-in access to law and finance circles. The Director cares most about profit, plain and simple. Where the money comes from doesn't matter much in the grand scheme of immortality; the Firm is as likely to deal with the mortal underworld as with tech conglomerates.
The Firm's power lies in intelligence and status, with occasional forays into hostile takeovers through outright force. Cutting its ties to the outside world injures its function.
In Chronicles
The Firm makes a point of being diplomatic with the Family and the Monarchy. It most loathes the Caligula as a source of unpredictability.
Talent Scout: The Firm's latest enterprise was a complete failure. The players' coterie is sent out to find promising new investors and convince them to ally with the dwindling Firm.
Gold Rush: The Firm has swallowed a rival domain in order to get a valuable resource. In the sudden gold rush for the material, how do the players' characters further their own agendas in a literally cutthroat economy?
King of the Hill: The coterie has the chance to become leaders of a small corner of the Firm. The only catch? They're in competition with another coterie, and the Firm wants to see who comes out on top.
The Collegium
The Collegium centers on shared faith. Whether their faith is religion, knowledge, or idealism varies from domain to domain. It operates on the basic idea that all members, especially leaders, uphold the same laws and customs. Kindred in these domains tend to be either immigrants to the region, drawn by particular tenets of the faith, or childer inducted by a sire who discovered a promising mortal. Followers give all but a few of their possessions to the Collegium.

16

COURTS OF THE DAMNED

In an academically inclined Collegium, leaders (generically called Chancellors) style themselves as professors or mentors. They value research: Chancellors might assign vampiric study projects or welcome redworkers, such as Thin-Blood Alchemists or Blood Sorcerers. Some declare certain criminal acts legal, so long as the accused demonstrates they learned something valuable by doing it. In religious Collegia, leaders emphasize the tenets of a particular religion: cleanliness, prayer, nights of fasting, and attendance at religious events. Violations of the faith are punished in the extreme. Public apologies to the entire Collegium are required for even minor slipups, and serious breaches result in banishment or final death. In the cruelest Collegia, a neonate's finger is cut off every night for a week for losing a rare book; for failing to appear at prayer, an ancilla whips herself until her spine peeks through.
A Chancellor in the Collegium is as absolute a ruler as the Monarch. Unlike their autocratic cousin, Chancellors have a covenant with a "higher power." This isn't misplaced belief on the part of their followers, either. Many Collegia are helmed by a Chancellor who truly believes in the sanctity of knowledge or has unshakable faith in Cainite doctrine. Their citizens are loyal precisely because of their leader's devotion. Corrupt Collegia ignore the doctrines they espouse to their followers. For them, the framework of the Collegium provides a way to use others' beliefs for their own gain. In either case, damaging their reputation hurts their base of power.
In Chronicles
The Collegium locks horns with the Firm (eschewing "mere profit" as beneath it). It dislikes the Family, recognizing a rival cult when it sees one, but might collaborate if circumstances require it.
Publish or Perish: The coterie comes into possession of evidence showing a Chancellor failed his vows. Do they sell it to the highest bidder, ask the Chancellor himself to pay, or distribute it to the Collegium as they see fit?

Top Secret: The coterie has been recruited to research a secret project within the Collegium. They must keep their work hidden from rival domains and the rest of the Collegium alike.
Confession: The coterie violated the Collegium's code. Do they confess and risk retribution, or cover up their crime?
The Family
"Mother and Father" (gender and relationship notwithstanding) sit at the head of the Family table. They direct their "sons and daughters" to act as lieutenants and enforcers--retaining final say on all business within their brood, of course. Usually, the "children" are quite literally Mother and Father's childer, although on occasion promising Kindred may be adopted into the family business. Each of the Family's offspring then creates their own block of childer or adoptees to be their officers. In fact, one key aspect of the Family is the ability to "marry into" a higher position by openly Blood Bonding with a more senior member of the Family.
The Family cares for its own. Each sire answers to their superiors on their childer's behalf, encouraging a closer relationship between the generations than in other Kindred power structures. Childer receive food, shelter, and familial comforts as long as they might need. But disputes within the Family turn nasty quickly as branches close ranks against each other. Outsiders caught in the Family's domain find themselves at the mercy of whichever lieutenant agrees to take them on as a responsibility, completely disregarded by Father or Mother until they're adopted or marry in. And if Father or his direct childer disappear, the system devolves into complete chaos. Given the family-tree structure, it's no surprise the Family is a favorite political framework for Tremere- and Hecata-controlled domains, and Gangrel territories tend to operate on similar terms.

17

In Chronicles
The Family distrusts most other domains. They might work with the Firm on occasion, enjoying the cash flow.
The Feud: Two branches of the Family have sworn to destroy each other on sight. The players' coterie is charged with negotiating between them. They must reach a compromise or else Mother steps in.
Inheritance: Father's been Beckoned, and he divides his domain between his childer before he departs. When the favored son gets all the prime real estate, his disgruntled siblings begin to move against him and each other.
Family Bonds: One of the Family's members defects from the domain. She asks the players' coterie to defend her since her Family is trying to get her back by any means necessary.
The Caligula
The Caligula follows no laws but rather its own desires. It insists its subjects have complete freedom to do as they will in its domain--provided they follow its personal commands to the letter. There's no law saying a group of mortals is off-limits to feed on, but the Caligula tells a random member of its domain she's not allowed to drink from drug dealers and tells another that he's forbidden to feed on all medical professionals--regardless of whether they routinely prey on such victims.
The Caligula rewards its enforcers heartily--and they tend to want to keep it in power as a result--but it's prone to promoting its favorites over the most capable. Rather than patrolling regularly,

COURTS OF THE DAMNED

its enforcers perform arbitrary spot checks on random vampires within their domain, telling their victims to perform tasks or prove their loyalty. If they refuse or fail, the punishment is usually lengthy and degrading. In this way, the city is kept in a perpetual sense of chaos, never sure of the capricious ruler's next move. The Caligula's framework is essentially based on raw force.
The Caligula's reign is also, predictably, short. After a few years, its subjects hit a breaking point and band together to drive it out or destroy it. Sometimes, if the Caligula is personified in an older Kindred with many friends in high places, it manages to last a shockingly long time.
In Chronicles
The Caligula conducts diplomacy with surrounding domains based on their perceived strength and opinion of it. It especially clashes with the Assembly and the Firm.
Gunpowder, Treason, and Plot: The coterie must infiltrate the Caligula's court and build support from within to overthrow it--all while the Caligula is watching them closely.
Best Friends for Now: The Caligula has decided it's delighted by the players' coterie. It showers them with attention and gifts. It did the same thing for its last favorite, too--before it grew suspicious of her and hunted her down with hounds and sabers.
The Assembly
Since the majority of world governance turned to democracy, the idea of a democratic government has taken root in Kindred society. Assemblies hold citywide votes led by a Kindred mayor and elect council members to govern them for a given period of time. Who gets blood isn't decided by decree but rather by vote--Kindred within an Assembly must petition to hunt in a certain area.
There's a shocking amount of red tape around voting practices and administrative positions, all designed to force immortal, ageless politicians into

ceding power at the appropriate times and places. At their most stable (and most stagnant, Assemblies are run by a rotating cast of backstabbing bureaucrats and aides who ascend the political ladder rung by painful rung. At their most unstable, one or two political ideologues can maneuver their followers into position and decide they're no longer interested in there being a vote at all. Some Assembly members dream of measured debates and general consensus, but it's a difficult ambition to achieve.
In Chronicles
The Assembly considers the Monarchy and the Caligula threats to its existence. The Firm is also heartily despised, while the Collegium and the Family may be allies or minor rivals.
Strongman: A demagogue is on the rise within an Assembly. His enforcers and followers exert pressure on the city's Kindred. Smaller groups form within the Assembly in order to deal with the threat. Each believes they can use the turmoil to build something better.
Town Hall: A meeting is called to deal with a pressing issue, but clashing personalities and fervent opinions stall everything. The coterie is summoned to corral the competing sides and get a vote through.
Election Night: It's time for a new crop of Assembly members to be elected. Each has a different agenda for the city, and each evaluates if they can turn the players' coterie into an asset.

19

Chapter One: Playing Politics

MECHANICS OF POLITICAL PLAY

The point of politics is to accumulate allies and resources and reduce enemies' power and holdings. Three strategic roles can help you understand some ways of achieving this: the Giver, the Builder, and the Destroyer. These aren't characters to be played--one player can vacillate between these roles several times during a chronicle or the course of a story.
The Giver isn't picky about which people they help out, working with several factions at once. They cast a wide net, leaving plenty of room for favors and resources from unexpected sources to fall in. The Giver uses their generosity as a tool to build a large, varied contact network, bouncing from contact to contact as needed. They rely on those contacts for protection against enemies or threats. If they fail, the Giver is out of luck.
The Builder focuses on a particular faction of people and builds that faction's influence, increasing their own power and reach by tying themselves to the group's fate. They seek out alliances to specifically aid their faction and focus on gathering resources for a particular project. If their group is strong, the Builder can amass enormous power and advance serious goals. If their group falls from grace, they fall with them.
The Destroyer takes the colder approach. They focus on a particular enemy, seeking out allies who mutually clash with their target. They broker deals with third parties to get more firepower against mutual enemies. If their target is particularly despised, they might find themselves accumulating quite a few assets--but once their enemy is taken out, they have to find a new strategy for accumulating or maintaining power.

Social Combat
All these strategies require social combat. Since the point of politics is to (mostly) avoid using force, social combat takes the place of most Physical tests in political spaces, giving the advantage to those who wield it most effectively. Political social combat comes down to three basic approaches: negotiation, intimidation, and reassurance. Negotiation is front and center in diplomatic disputes--who gets what, what they'll give for it, and when they'll hand it over. Intimidation furnishes the why and how of these exchanges, usually by threatening to end political discussion and descend into conflict. Reassurance serves as intimidation's sweeter sibling, allowing allies to trade and deal with each other by promising good behavior from all involved.
As with all social combat situations, remember that fewer dice rolls are usually better. Not only does this keep players from getting tapped out--it's easier to come up with exciting action sequences or supernatural effects than convincing arguments on the fly--but it also tends to reflect the real world: people are either ready to have their mind changed, or they're not. In conversation between political rivals or allies, using the One-Roll Conflict system (Vampire, p. 298) usually suffices to determine whether players succeed or fail in influencing someone. If the troupe plays out an adversarial confrontation, try to keep to the Three Rounds and Out structure (Vampire, p. 295). This gives enough time for one side to act, their target to respond, and a final roll to wrap up the exchange.

20

COURTS OF THE DAMNED

CONSIDERATE SOCIAL CONFLICT Playing scenes of social combat, or just conflict, can be a very different experience from playing out violence and Discipline use. When we're sitting at the table, we don't get up to duke it out on the carpet. This creates a natural distance between the story and what's actually happening. But when we play out scenes of social combat, we are saying the things our characters say, maybe even yelling at each other. The distance between a player and their character is smaller, which can make it less obvious who the angry party is in that moment--the player or the character? Here are some quick tips to help make the distinction clear:  Use character names frequently when speaking in character.  Introduce yelling and similar outbursts with third-person narrative, so it becomes "Victoria is furious and
exclaims, `You contribute nothing to this group!'" not "You contribute nothing to this group."  Include character habits and mannerisms, any particular compulsive ones that the character has, when
portraying them. Perhaps the character laughs in a way the player does not.  Target character weaknesses; avoid known player insecurities.  Feel free to take a short break after confrontational scenes, to laugh it out. Or talk about them after the
session, if you prefer to avoid interruptions, and compliment each other on what was cool. · Pay attention to each other, so you notice the use of any calibration techniques (such as Lines and Veils, the
Stoplight System, and others you'll find in Vampire pp. 423­425). Note that these aren't only for players. Storytellers are also allowed to want a change of direction.

No matter the social combat structure, there are dozens of ways to use Social and Mental Skills within a situation. Troupes may determine their own dice pools as appropriate, but some suggestions can be seen in Table 1: Social Combat, which demonstrates how Storytellers might vary the combinations of likely dice pools.
Setting the Goal
The defining element of any combat, whether physical or social, is knowing what you're trying to achieve--after all, without that goal firmly in mind, you're just having a heated conversation.
Before entering into any social combat scenario, consider how your goal aligns with the following options:  Inception: The most devious forms of social
combat are those that the targets don't even realize happened. The goal here is to implant a suggestion without it seeming to come from you--the perfect crime.  Muckraking: Your opponent has something you want, and they're holding out on you. Attempts at muckraking are all about teasing out some secret or another, whether through

coercion, empathy, or a forced slip of the tongue.  Point of View: You hold a perspective your
opponent disagrees with and refuses to accept, and that just can't be. This combat involves each party trying to convince the other to see things from their point of view--they may not leave the conversation in full agreement, but they gain at least some understanding.  Self-Defense: When you're attacked, you fight back with whatever you've got. This approach is less of a goal and more of an instinctive reaction, but the winning result is the same--you stand up for yourself without revealing any cracks in your armor.  Undermining: Someone needs to be taken down a peg or two, and you're the exact bloodsucker to do it. Most attempts at undermining focus on the two (or more) characters directly involved in the social combat, each seeking to humiliate the other. It isn't limited to just the participants--the combat could be staged to elicit a reaction from the audience or even ruin the reputation of an uninvolved third party.

Redefining the Risks
You know why you're fighting, but what are you risking? If you come out on top, what happens to your opponent? Social combat typically inflicts Willpower damage, but this needn't be your only option. In fact, many groups might feel hesitant to even attempt social combat, knowing that a large portion of that precious (and hard to recover) resource is at stake.
Before entering social combat, the group can look over this list of alternative options and decide what's appropriate. Crucially, both player and Storyteller need to agree on the stakes before the combat begins.
Backgrounds: Allies, Contacts, Retainers, Resources, Status--there's a long list of assets a character could leverage to win an argument. Of course, that means they're at risk of being impacted. These losses need not be directly referenced in the conversation. Perhaps other Kindred learn of the discussion and take advantage of the perceived weakness, or maybe the brooding vampire temporarily alienates their allies as they obsess over their loss. Background dots affected in this manner are equivalent to the amount of Willpower that would have been lost and remain unavailable for the rest of the session for Superficial damage or story (typically two to three sessions) for Aggravated damage.
The Beast's Due: Commonly, when someone's ego is being taunted, here the loser risks Frenzy (Terror or Fury could both be appropriate). At the end of each turn of combat, the loser rolls to resist Frenzy. The Difficulty begins at 1 and increases by 1 for each point of Willpower damage they would have taken (to a maximum of 7). Onlookers can notice the barely restrained emotion with an Intelligence + Awareness or Wits + Insight test at Difficulty 2.
Compulsions and Stains: The character obsesses over their loss, losing themselves in the distraction of their failure. Afflicting them with a suitable Compulsion is a great way to represent the vampiric undertones that drive this tantrum, but a Stain (or two) as the vampire lashes out in Tenet-violating actions can also be appropriate.

Faustian Pact: Both sides agree to strictly follow the results of the combat; if one side is convincing, the losers walk away in full agreement and change their plans accordingly. While this might seem obvious, this agreement ensures everyone knows their hard work is backed up by an (out-of-character) ironclad oath.
Secrets and Lies: Everyone has something they want to know, so why not put it on the table? The loser in this social combat discloses a secret they'd prefer to keep hidden (although they may not even recognize their slip if the interrogation was subtle enough).
Social Standing: Kindred society may be ruthless, but it's still a society, and with the Masquerade in full force, vampires are forced to stick closely to their own kind to be themselves. Losing face, being seen as untrustworthy, and, of course, abject humiliation can see you forcibly pulled down the social ladder.
Any loss in social standing can be represented by penalties to Status or a dice pool when interacting with other Kindred (a two-dice penalty is normal, while penalties of three dice are suitable for spectacular failures). These persist until someone else embarrasses themselves and distracts the masses. Interestingly, those who experience a loss of status within one group may find their social cachet increasing with the opposition.
WILLPOWER LOSS IN SOCIAL COMBAT As well as the standard rules for Willpower loss during social combat, players may prefer to set a limit on how much they're willing to lose before they concede (even tying it to one of the options above). Another alternative is the all-or-nothing approach: here, the combat proceeds as usual, and at the end of each turn, the loser records how much Willpower damage they would have taken. At the end of the three rounds (or longer, if you're particularly masochistic), both sides compare how much damage they've accrued; the ultimate loser takes all the damage they had stored, while the winner takes none.

24

COURTS OF THE DAMNED

NAVIGATING THE POLITICAL
LANDSCAPE

From when they first meet others like themselves, Vampire characters become mired in a complicated web, where every move can have far-reaching, unknown implications and consequences. They either learn to act with purpose or become pawns in someone else's game. Camarilla Courts (see Chapter Two: The Ivory Tower) are mired in a byzantine tradition of patronage and influence, with older vampires vying

for position and power built on centuries of rivalry. Anarch stomping grounds (see Chapter Three: The Movement) run on a system of favors and bespoke laws, building up and tearing down leaders at lightning speed. Every inch of established Kindred territory has been the subject of countless political machinations over the years. It's possible to eschew politics entirely, but turning a blind eye means having little say over the law when the law starts having a say over you.

DAYLIGHTERS The highs and lows of vampire politics are fertile ground, but don't forget--the Kindred exist within an urban landscape rife with countless mortal complaints and political rivalries of its own. Mortals can move during the day and stay awake all night if needed. With some preparation, a group of ordinary, tough mortals is a match for any thin-blood, fledgling, or neonate vampire. With a great deal of preparation, they're equal to most ancillae and (sometimes) older generations. On the political side, mortals need no Blush of Life to keep other mortals from getting suspicious. They have no Banes, no Beast, and no Hunger to distract them. They can buy out Kindred companies and rezone domain territory. Neighborhood watches can disrupt meetings and alert law enforcement. The right mortal ally in high places can be a powerful tool in a vampire's arsenal.
Just remember, no Kindred leader is powerful enough to keep an entire kine government in his sway. This extends to various mortal systems too. A vampire cannot affect a city's entire network of churches, pharmacies, or colleges with their Influence or Allies, unless that city is more of a village. Rather than simply declaring "I have Restaurant Influence so I can find out X and Y," using Influence should be concrete: "I email Chef George; he can set up a meeting with this restaurant owner for us." Mortal Influence and Allies aren't simply there to solve problems, but they can give the coterie an opening to solve them on their own.

25

Chapter One: Playing Politics

Securing a Niche
The first and most important rule of politics is this: figure out how to make yourself essential. Give others no choice but to associate value with you, or they'll consider you dead weight at best, and a dangerous waste of blood at worst. The easiest way for players' characters to do this is to find a niche and protect it with everything they've got. Be it a connection at the city morgue, a talent for entertainment, connections to organized crime, or a knack for making deliveries faster than anyone, claim it, advertise it, and defend it.
Once you have it, grow it. A good musician or a fast courier tonight should be brilliant or legendary a decade from now, or else they'll be left behind in the dust by the next ambitious Kindred. If your coterie is moving in on another's territory, outperform them and outlast them. The ideal takeover isn't a battle but a merger: turn your rivals into allies, and they'll be competition no longer.
The players' characters are not the only ones thinking of their talents. Their sires most likely Embraced them for a reason. Even a sire motivated by guilt or loneliness noticed some quality of character that convinced him to request an Embrace or make a dangerous gamble. A sire might be a character's first political ally, willing to help the coterie protect their niche to preserve his childe's talents.
Identifying Your Targets
You can't sell out without customers. A coterie may present as a new bud on the Camarilla vine, declare themselves as Anarch assets, be on the lookout for the highest bidder, or pretend at neutrality. No matter what, as long as they control access to anything at all, they're shifting the balance of resources in someone's favor. The trick is to figure out whose and ensure that they reward you for it.

A part of this is understanding the political landscape that you're trying to navigate--who holds a grudge against whom? What happened in 1982, since nobody allows references to that year within earshot of the Court? Whom should you absolutely not be seen with if you want to be trusted? Knowing how the existing Kindred are connected is an essential element in understanding what they want and why they want it.
Understanding Your Assets
A deal is being struck. It's time for a player's character to hand over their half of the goods. If they've got it, cash, territory, or information might be the way to go. But what if their wallet is empty and they've got no data to trade during negotiations?
If immediate resources aren't on the table, offering the right skill set and willingness to get into the weeds can be a powerful bargaining chip. There's always a demand for unpleasant, difficult, timeconsuming, or dangerous tasks to be handled. For organized crime or corrupt mortal politicians, a set of Blood-enhanced Skills and a clean Mask are catnip. For older Kindred, the prospect of a relatively unknown agent can be compelling. Offer Skills like they're a resume and back them up with evidence you can deliver.
Under the right circumstances, even Flaws can be exploited. Begging for a favor might be humiliating, but it can stroke the ego of the person being petitioned. After all, letting others know your weaknesses might serve them down the road. Borrowing from the future by setting yourself up to fail or promising favors you're not sure you can deliver on might not be wise, but politicians do it all the time. The trick is to manage future risks, and sometimes that future never comes--say, if the Kindred you're in debt to is destroyed before they can collect.

26

COURTS OF THE DAMNED

Remaining Flexible
Targets move and change, especially for those trying to climb up from the bottom of the career ladder, and services must change as well. Sometimes a character finds themselves with their back to the wall, fangs at their throat. Then they'll need to think quickly and give their enemy a reason not to destroy them. A vampire wanting to thrive in the political sphere probably needs to be able to swallow their pride and do the unthinkable every once in a while.
And when they've got someone up against the wall, they should remember that enemies--particularly predictable enemies--can become assets with the careful application of mercy. Just don't trust them to watch your back.
Working with Others
Allies and Enemies
Kindred politics play out among a relatively small number of individuals. In many domains, getting

just three or four vampires to work together already marks the beginning of a promising coalition. A smaller pool of potential allies means Kindred are often willing to accept partners whose goals are partly at cross-purposes to their own. Unfortunately, this raises the chances of making enemies as well.
What makes an enemy? Someone working against you. Someone untrustworthy. Someone who just can't stand you. There doesn't have to be a grand reason behind animosity--people are petty and unkind to those they dislike, and more than a few enjoy rubbing salt into wounds.
Which is precisely why mutual enemies (and the destruction thereof) are one of the classic motivators for alliance. "The enemy of my enemy" may not be an outright friend, but they're useful to have in the coterie's corner. When their shared opponent is safely ground down into a paste, the allies may forge a lasting friendship built on their shared experiences--or they may break apart and become enemies themselves. Such is the nature of political deals.

27

Chapter One: Playing Politics

What makes a good ally? Someone with aligned goals. Someone with something valuable to offer. Someone trustworthy. Someone who isn't an unbearable asshole. Of course, most potential allies don't check every one of those boxes. Most of them check maybe two at best. But any combination of those four qualities is worth looking into as a potential friend or partner.
Once a likely ally is found, the next step is figuring out what they want and need. The two aren't necessarily the same. Needs are fleeting, shifting with changing circumstances. Wants change, too, but they tend to be much longerlasting--artistic fulfillment, love, fame, and so on. Doing someone a need-based favor is a good first step to earning their trust, but allying based on wants creates a stronger, more genuine relationship. Two Kindred seeking to bring a common enemy to justice will work harder to stick together than two searching for a place to crash.
Building Coalitions
Once just two or three political allies have joined forces, they need some ground rules. Building a successful coalition necessitates ironing out differences until everyone can focus on the mutual goal. Define the endgame: a faction's control of the city, the final death of an enemy, the fall of a particular luminary. Set a few nonnegotiables for all involved--"don't touch X," "don't work with Y," "keep Z safe." If one ally has a do-or-die demand the others don't particularly care about, it still goes on the list--otherwise, that ally won't see the reason to stick around.
Finally, determine the most important part of any coalition: who handles what. This doesn't necessarily mean deciding on a leader but rather delegating key tasks to specific members and agreeing that no one else will interfere to muddle their plans. Responsibilities might include recruiting new members, gathering intelligence, securing resources, spreading information, or acquiring rewards for coalition allies. If priorities

change, the coalition must adapt its structure to meet new circumstances or risk breaking apart under outside pressure.
Appearing to Comply
If your schemes run counter to your city's leadership, you'll need to move carefully. Outright civil disobedience might work under certain circumstances. For instance if you have a strong union of mortal workers to back you up in what appears to them a mortal issue or if you're simply old and strong enough to get your way. Other times, it's safer to do one thing while appearing to do another.
Creative obedience can be a potent advantage, especially when dealing with a Firm or an Assembly. You might engage in malicious compliance, following the letter of the law so strictly that it becomes counterproductive. Perhaps you go above and beyond to do more than you were asked in ways that serve your cause, or you find reasons to stretch out a task way past its usefulness. A coalition that appears to obey can be difficult for an unsatisfied ruler to deal with, especially if they're popular within the domain and any crackdown would carry a heavy political cost.
Handling Setbacks
Plans collapse, resources dry up, allies flake. Political setbacks can feel like total failure, but seasoned players know they're part of the game.
Start by asking: "Was this a setback for me, for my coterie, or for our alliances?" These overlap, but they're not the same. A personal setback--the loss of a patron, a fall from grace, a rival's triumph--affects the individual hardest. It can strain relationships, but the fallout is usually containable. These are best handled through personal action: an apology, a favor, a fight. In fact, recovering gracefully can win more respect than never stumbling at all.
Coterie or alliance setbacks have a wider impact. When everyone's affected, there's less stability

28

COURTS OF THE DAMNED

to fall back on. The crisis might bring the group closer together, but it also risks splitting it apart. The key is to hold on tight to allies, keep doing favors and (perhaps counterintuitively) showing generosity to keep everyone happy, and control the narrative. Consider how you want the situation to look to outsiders. Do you want your group to look like sympathetic underdogs, beaten down by an undeserving rival? Youthful fools? Or as strong as ever? Sometimes you can even get away with reframing failure as a win.
Setbacks are a perfect time to rethink your approach. If a particular tack failed, consider paths not taken. If subtlety fell flat, strike boldly. If a partnership with a certain Kindred ended in embarrassment, turn the fallout into opportunity by playing the wry ex to his rivals, now all too familiar with an old flame's nonsense. In politics, making it isn't about never failing but about how you recover.

scandal or a major crime that the target can plausibly be linked to. The narrative must answer how, why, and where, and should be backed by witnesses and planted evidence; leave just enough wiggle room in to account for minor discrepancies.
The alibi is the most crucial element of this charade. In a world where Disciplines can force the truth out of unwilling subjects, ensuring the coterie isn't questioned to begin with is ideal. The nextbest thing is creating an easily corroborated cover story for everyone. Most people don't remember everything about a particular night, so reciting the exact same story is suspicious. Instead, focus on a few key details like approximate time, conversation topics, or what was playing on TV wherever the coterie was totally, honestly spending the night.
Finally, decide whether the target should realize they were set up. Humiliation can be a delicious way to intimidate rivals, but it also creates a grudge that can last centuries.

Taking the Fall
Sometimes a crisis reaches the critical point, and there's no one left to blame who isn't an ally. Someone must take the fall to give the rest a way to wriggle free and continue their work another night. It's a nasty job. At best, it leads to social ostracization; at worst, final death. If someone steps up--or draws the short straw--the group must manage their response carefully. If the scapegoat is banished, they should protect his interests while he's gone or risk him returning as an enemy. If he's outright destroyed, they must move cautiously, lest their enemies whisper to those in power that they're plotting revenge.
Frame Job
Better than letting one of the coterie's own take the fall is pinning the crime on an enemy. A frame job needs a few things: a problem to blame on an enemy, a believable narrative, and a clear alibi for the players' characters. The first element is often easy to come by; either you already have it, or you cook up a

Taking Power
The coterie has gathered allies, done favors for Kindred in high and low places, and accumulated boons from important figures across the city. The time is right to strike. It's their moment to make a play for power.
The key to a successful power play is convincing one's enemy that surrender serves their survival better than resistance. The coterie must have equal or greater force at its disposal than whomever they intend to depose. Force needn't be physical--coteries can leverage public opinion or the support of their allies, threaten financial ruin, or make use of blackmail.
Mechanically, count each reasonable Ally and Resource at the coterie's disposal as one extra die in social combat against their opponent. This is a one-time bonus for that confrontation. The allies lend strength for now but won't let the coterie trade endlessly on their goodwill.

29

Chapter One: Playing Politics
Any coterie trying to seize power must remember their strength isn't purely for show. If they fail in social combat, their opponent concludes they were bluffing or weak-willed. The characters then have to follow up on their threats. If they regroup to return another night, their opponent will likely attempt to take advantage of their weakness. Erase any positive connections between the two parties on the Political Map (p. 32­34)--the nights ahead are about to get far more dangerous.
PLAYING POLITICS BADLY The advice in this section is all about how to carry out political plays that give the players' characters advantages in the complicated backstabbing of Kindred power games. But politics isn't only for the skilled. Sometimes it can be immense fun to roleplay as an incompetent politician (or as someone pretending to be more incompetent than they are while their allies discreetly carry out their master plan). These are some ideas for actions you can take if you want to play politics badly:  Publicly declare your plans.  Offer boons you can't or won't fulfill.  Try to blackmail someone using publicly known information.  Contradict yourself.  Switch sides mid-meeting because "both arguments sound convincing."  Insult your allies.  Lie loudly and obviously about your abilities.  Change allegiances every time there's a shift in public opinion.  Work hard to get a post you're not qualified for and do none of the work it comes with. · Get caught breaking minor domain rules all the time, as you consider yourself above them.
Biryakov: No, please. They sent me to help you... Kevin Jackson: I'm sure they did. They told you that you'd be recognized by the Prince and stay in the city if you did this little favor. Biryakov: Yes. Yes, please. I'm just trying to be useful. Kevin Jackson: This city is my domain, little rat. You want to stay here, you come to me. It doesn't matter if you're a Nosferatu or not. I wasn't born to the throne. I've seen every aspect of this city. You think you can play games with me, think again. Biryakov: Please, good Prince, no games... Kevin Jackson: So why are we here? Biryakov: It's a trap, sir. I don't know who they really are, but -- Kevin Jackson: They told you that if their Prince were to replace me, it'd be no trouble at all to recognize you. Biryakov: Yes... Kevin Jackson: Let's go spring that trap. Afterwards, I'll think about what to do with you.
Prince Kevin Jackson of Chicago lays down the law for the Nosferatu Biryakov

COURTS OF THE DAMNED

CREATING A POLITICAL CHRONICLE

Political play, at its core, is about personal connections and hard choices. Every political epic wrestles with trading morality for power, losing friends and family, and--in the end--possibly becoming a monster to achieve greatness. In other words, a perfect vampire story.
In some chronicles, politics might be the engine of a Camarilla Prince's Court--a series of delicate alliances and ancient agreements trying to preserve stability. In others, it's the weapon the players' coterie wields to carve a new Anarch stronghold into their city. Politics offers endless plots and subplots, from assassinations and rags-to-riches stories in thrillers like The Day of the Jackal, All the King's Men, or Z, to the petty feuds and dark satire of The Death of Stalin, The Sympathizer, or Succession.
Making Politics Work for You
Players may wonder how their personal Ambitions (Vampire, pp. 173­74) can fit into a political chronicle. Have no fear: there is ample room to follow even the wildest character dreams. The simplest way is to align political goals with existing Ambitions. A Brujah with plans to overthrow Camarilla control seeks out Anarch allies and helps

them rise. A Ventrue with a personal vendetta against a rival focuses on publicly humiliating him before his superiors. Troupes beginning a political chronicle should encourage one another to link Ambitions to specific people or groups on the Relationship Map.
For characters with less political Ambitions, folding personal goals into political decisions can create strong roleplaying moments. A Toreador striving to achieve Golconda argues passionately for a humane approach to conflict. A Lasombra hunting for her elder's lost library convinces her coterie to lay claim to contested territory that might conceal it. Rather than waiting for a story to serve their Ambitions, players can make their goals the reason as well as the reward for the decisions their characters make. That library-seeking Lasombra won't just fulfill her own Ambition by taking over the library--it's also a potential prize to share with allies who join her search.

31

Chapter One: Playing Politics

Building the Political Map
A Political Map is a politically tuned chart of who's who in a city's power structure. It's essentially a Relationship Map of the city and its denizens. It functions very much like a redworking Scene Map (Blood Sigils, p. 48), highlighting important people and ticking-time-bomb rivalries. You can draw it up separately from your Relationship Map, using different colors for different factions, or add it directly to your Relationship Map in one bright, unique color.
Creating a Political Map as a troupe is a great way to start a political chronicle. Players are more invested in the world when they've helped shape it. Instead of asking the Storyteller, "Who's in charge of this?" or "Do I know about that faction?" they can plan and scheme, knowing everyone shares the same basic foundation.

For Storytellers, it's also an exciting way to create a political landscape, as it ensures multiple factions with conflicting agendas share the same stage. Letting players take the lead here can be a relaxing experience for Storytellers who dislike preemptively guessing what players will enjoy.
The player-driven approach doesn't fit every game. Adding a new player to a political chronicle means plunging their character into an alreadyspinning machine of intrigue and names. Rather than building out the Political Map with new, disconnected threads, the troupe can let them choose which faction or character they want a connection with. Similarly, for short chronicles, one-shots stories, new troupes, or troupes playing fledglings in over their heads, Storytellers may prefer to design the city's structure themselves. Drawing out a Political Map is also a great way for Storytellers to figure out a legible presentation of the city's power structure. For more Storyteller advice, see Chapter Five: The Storyteller's Manifesto.

COURTS OF THE DAMNED

Step One:
The Center of the Web
A character is a player's entrance into a story, so build your Political Map with the players' characters at its center. This doesn't mean a whole domain revolves around them--rather, it represents their forays into various conflicts and rivalries around the city. As with a Relationship Map, write the players' characters' names down first. Then jot down a short note that describes their place in the city's political sphere: "enforcer for my sire," "spy for the Anarchs," or "complete newbie," for instance.
It's completely acceptable for characters to start with no background in Kindred power struggles or connections to existing factions. Write them on the map anyway. As the chronicle unfolds, they'll form links to the city's major players.

immediately suggests itself, don't worry about linking them to the coterie just yet (this applies to subsequent steps as well). If a player wishes, they can skip creating a major power and instead create two minor powers.
Some troupes might like to assign individual clans to specific players, for instance letting the Tremere of the coterie decide how the Tremere fit into the city's political landscape.
Occasionally, a player might propose a major power that doesn't fit the chronicle's tone or themes. The troupe can suggest some tweaks to integrate it better, but sometimes an idea just won't work. In such cases, the Storyteller might reframe it as a minor power or ask the player to think of something else. Since the Storyteller plays these characters during the game, it's important that they also see their narrative and thematic potential.

Step Two:
The Major Powers
Major powers can be anything or anyone wielding significant influence over a key resource (see "The Stakes," p. 12). Examples include an influential Kindred leader, a powerful enforcer, an up-andcoming gang taking over a blood-rich district, and a dominant information broker. If there is a Prince or Baron, the Storyteller adds them to the map at the beginning of this step.
Each player gets to add one major power to the map. Give them a name, a clan if appropriate, and a few words of description. Note which resource they primarily control. Unless a particular relationship

Step Three:
The Minor Powers
Minor powers have some influence and ability but don't want or haven't achieved major power. They might be aides, informants, low-level enforcers, loyal Retainers--the list goes on. Don't shy away from including non-Kindred characters. As in Step Two, each player gets the chance to add a minor power to the map, giving them a name, a clan if relevant, and a few words of description.

33

Chapter One: Playing Politics

Step Four:
Potential Missing Pieces
Look at your Relationship Map and character sheets. Are there Storyteller characters left who ought to be on the Political Map? Add them in with a short description of their position and any relevant links to connections from the Relationship Map.  Sires and Mawali likely hold political allegiances.
Whether a player's character shares those allegiances is a great source of tension or support.  Allies, Contacts, and Retainers may have indirect connections in Kindred politics or access to mortal institutions, giving players' characters extra leverage.  Touchstones probably have no role in Kindred politics but can become targets if rivals decide to use them as a bargaining chip.  At least one or two Enemies and Adversaries (if the coterie's got any) should be on the map, too. Their placement doesn't have to mirror the players' characters' relationship to them--an Enemy working for the same boss as the coterie may be more interesting than an Adversary in a rival faction.
Step Five:
Alliances and Rivalries
Political play requires connections between characters to create juicy drama. Each player creates one alliance and one rivalry between characters on the map, including the players' characters. Draw

a line between them and jot down a short phrase describing the relationship: "Reluctant allies against Harrison." "Enemies since the '60s." "Everyone knows Elle is Park's spymaster." For the major powers, it's a good idea to add a direct connection to the domain's leader(s). If one alliance and one rivalry per player seems like too few, go for a second round.
Players' characters can be connected to the same Storyteller characters, and it can be an excellent way to establish a relationship--perhaps weaving it into your characters' opinions of each other.
Step Six:
Pressure
Some alliances and rivalries are stable and longlasting. Those involved know each other well and anticipate each other's moves. Others burn hot and move fast--alliances bloom and wither; enmities ignite and explode.
Choose three alliances or rivalries between Storyteller characters. Mark a series of boxes next to them in a contrasting color to represent Pressure--the strain politics and diplomacy exert on relationships. The most stable relationships get six boxes. New, tenuous alliances or rivalries get four. And volatile connections only get two. Pressure can dominate a story, or it can simmer in the background. Either way, it highlights the relationships the players can manipulate, mend, or push toward collapse in their chronicle.
The Storyteller can add new Pressure boxes to the Political Map whenever it feels appropriate. If

Table 2: Sources of Pressure
Event Regular passage of time. Players' coterie undermines or aids one side. One side is a major player or plot element in a session. One side's goal is achieved. A new connection is added to one side. One side is disgraced or destroyed.

Pressure Tick 1 Pressure box per story. Tick 1 Pressure box per session. Tick 1 Pressure box per session. Tick 2 Pressure boxes per story. Add 1 Pressure box per session. Instant fulfillment.

a new source of tension or strain emerges during play--especially if caused by player action--record it on the map. The more the coterie interacts with a particular Storyteller character, the quicker the Pressure boxes next to that connection fill. There's no hard limit to how much Pressure can exist on the map, but remember that too many relationships hitting their boiling point at once can be tricky to roleplay. Use your judgment.
When all the Pressure boxes for a connection fill, the connection is removed. One side takes action against the other, whether it's to attack them, ice them out, or declare their mutual goal fulfilled. It's always a change noticed and commented on by Kindred across the city. A new connection can be drawn in the next session if appropriate.
